Spectrum of the Maxwell Equations for a Flat Interface between Non-Homogeneous Dispersive Media in 2D and 3D
Tomáš Dohnal, Michael Plum, Karl M. Schmidt, and Ian Wood

Abstract
The present work concerns the time-harmonic Maxwell equations in two- and three-dimensional space, divided into two half-spaces by a flat interface. The two half-spaces are filled with media whose electric permittivity is frequency-dependent and varies as a function of the distance from the interface only; this dependence is assumed to satisfy a regularity condition in each half-space but may be discontinuous at the interface. No specific model for the frequency dependence is assumed. For the associated operator pencil, we characterise subsets of the resolvent set and separate subsets of the Weyl spectrum corresponding to radiation away from the interface and along the interface, respectively. The characterisation of the sets is via conditions on the fundamental solutions. If the media are periodic in the direction orthogonal to the interface, a more explicit description of these sets can be given in terms of Floquet theory of related Sturm-Liouville equations. These results generalise earlier work in one and two dimensions where the media were assumed to be homogeneous in each half-space.

Keywords
: Maxwell equations, operator pencils, spectrum, Weyl sequences, dispersive material, interface, inhomogeneous media

1
Introduction

We study the spectrum of an operator pencil corresponding to the time-harmonic Maxwell problem in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with an interface between two non-homogeneous and dispersive (i.e. frequency dependent) materials. The interface is given by the plane
x_{1}=0
and the two media’s electromagnetic properties are dependent only on
x_{1}
(and the frequency
\omega
) but independent of
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
. This paper is a continuation of
[
7
]
where the problem in
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with an interface between two homogeneous (
x-
independent) media was analyzed.

In the second-order formulation for the spatial profile
E
of the electric field, and setting the permeability equal to one, Maxwell’s equations for dispersive materials in
\mathbb{R}^{N},N=2,3,
in the frequency domain are

\nabla\times\nabla\times E-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)E=0,\quad\nabla\cdot(\perm E)=0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{N},

(1.1)

where in our interface setting

\perm(x_{1},\omega)=\begin{cases}\perm_{-}(x_{1},\omega),&x_{1}<0,\\
\perm_{+}(x_{1},\omega),&x_{1}>0.\end{cases}

(1.2)

We assume

D(\perm):=\{\omega\in\mathbb{C}:\perm_{\pm}(\cdot,\omega)\in W^{3,\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm})\}\neq\emptyset,

(1.3)

where
\mathbb{R}_{+}:=(0,\infty)
and
\mathbb{R}_{-}:=(-\infty,0)
.
In the case
N=2
we additionally assume that
E
is independent of
x_{3}
. Note that possible singularities of
\omega\mapsto\perm(\cdot,\omega)
lie outside of
D(\perm)
. Such singularities are common in physical models, like the Drude or Lorentz models, see (
1.10
) and (
1.11
). Our spectral analysis covers the case of
\perm
with singularities.

We view the left hand side of (
1.1
) as the operator pencil
L(\omega):=-\omega^{2}\perm(\cdot,\omega)+\nabla\times\nabla\times
acting on
E
. Note that in
[
7
]
we had
\perm_{\pm}
independent of
x
and studied the cases
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We make no assumptions on the
\omega
-dependence of
\perm_{\pm}
. In particular, the dependence can be nonlinear and
\perm_{\pm}
can be complex-valued, which is the case in non-conservative (typically dissipative) media. The resulting operator pencil is then non-self-adjoint and nonlinear in the spectral parameter
\omega
.

The interface induces localization of the field, see the well-known effect of surface plasmons
[
25
,
23
,
16
,
4
]
, where the first two references are from the physics literature. However, full two- or three-dimensional localization is not expected as there is no mechanism for localization in directions parallel to the interface.
By allowing the permittivities
\perm_{\pm}
to depend on
x_{1}
, we make it possible to consider, e.g., the interface between two materials of which one is a photonic crystal. Wave propagation in photonic crystals built from dispersive materials is of physical interest, see, e.g.,
[
24
,
3
]
. Also, surface plasmons are often studied on surfaces of gratings or crystals made of dispersive materials, see, e.g., chapter 3 in
[
20
]
.

The spectrum of the dispersive Maxwell problem has been previously studied also by other authors. A linear non-self-adjoint pencil problem was considered in
[
18
,
2
]
. A nonlinear non-self-adjoint pencil in a cavity was studied in
[
17
]
. Recent works on the spectrum of the Maxwell operator with dispersive media include
[
12
,
14
,
15
]
. For more background on operator pencils, their use in modeling physical problems and their spectra, we refer to
[
21
,
22
]
as well as the introduction in
[
7
]
and the references therein. Closely related to our work are the papers
[
8
,
9
,
11
]
where the wave propagation at a flat interface between a dispersive medium and vacuum is investigated in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In
[
11
]
also a dispersive slab inside vacuum is considered. In all cases of
[
8
,
9
,
11
]
the media are, however, homogeneous, and
\perm_{\pm}(\omega)
is given by a specific non-dissipative Drude model, leading to a self-adjoint problem.

In
[
7
]
, where
\perm_{\pm}(\omega)
were constants, we divided the spectrum into the part outside the singular set

\Omega_{0}:=\{\omega\in D(\perm):\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(\omega)=0\ \text{or}\ \omega^{2}\perm_{-}(\omega)=0\}

(1.4)

and the part in
\Omega_{0}
. In
\Omega_{0}
the pencil
L(\omega)
reduces to the operator
\nabla\times\nabla\times
and features special spectral properties. Also, the set

M:=\{\omega\in D(\perm):\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\ \text{or}\ \omega^{2}\perm_{-}(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}

(1.5)

plays a special role in
[
7
]
- it lies in the essential spectrum since plane wave solutions of (
1.1
) exist if
\omega\in M
.

In the current paper, where
\perm_{\pm}
depend on
x_{1}
, the coefficients
\omega^{2}\perm_{\pm}(\cdot,\omega)
can be in
[0,\infty)
or arbitrarily close to
[0,\infty)
on a subset of
\mathbb{R}_{\pm}
. It is not clear to us what such values of
\omega
mean for the spectrum.
In most of the paper we work in the complement of the following exceptional sets

\displaystyle\Omega^{+}_{a}=\{\omega\in D(\perm):\mbox{\rm dist}(\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\omega),\{a\})=0\},

(1.6)

\displaystyle\Omega^{-}_{a}=\{\omega\in D(\perm):\mbox{\rm dist}(\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(\mathbb{R}_{-},\omega),\{a\})=0\}

for some suitably chosen
a\geq 0
or in the complement of the set

\Omega:=\{\omega\in D(\perm):\mbox{\rm dist}(\omega^{2}\perm(\mathbb{R},\omega),\mathbb{R}_{+})=0\}.

(1.7)

Note that the exceptional set
\Omega
is a generalization of
\Omega_{0}\cup M
.
Only for the example of
\perm_{\pm}
independent of
x
, we determine the spectrum on the whole set
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Omega_{0}
.

1.1
The Mathematical Setting

Here, we explain the origin of equation (
1.1
) and the functional analytic setting in which we study the spectrum. We also review the definitions of the spectrum and some of its parts for the case of operator pencils. For a more detailed discussion of this see
[
7
]
.

The time-harmonic Maxwell equations in
\mathbb{R}^{N},N=2,3,
in the absence of free charges and free currents and with a constant permeability take the form

\displaystyle\nabla\times E

\displaystyle={\rm i}\omega\boldsymbol{\mu}H,

(1.8)

\displaystyle\nabla\times H

\displaystyle=-{\rm i}\omega D,\quad D=\perm(x_{1},\omega)E,

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot D

\displaystyle=0,\quad\nabla\cdot H=0,

where

\nabla:=(\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}},\partial_{x_{3}})^{\top}\ \text{if}\ N=3\qquad\text{and}\qquad\nabla:=(\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}},0)^{\top}\ \text{if}\ N=2.

\perm
is the electric permittivity of the medium and
\boldsymbol{\mu}>0
is the magnetic permeability of the material. In the rest of the paper we set, for the sake of brevity,

\boldsymbol{\mu}=1.

This can be achieved by rescaling
H
and the variable
x
. This simplification has no effect on the results. We assume that
\perm
depends on space only through the variable
x_{1}
.

Equation (
1.8
) arises from the time-dependent dispersive Maxwell equations by the generalized time-harmonic ansatz

(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{H})(x,t)=(E,H)(x)e^{-{\rm i}\omega t}+(\overline{E},\overline{H})(x)e^{{\rm i}\overline{\omega}t},\omega\in\mathbb{C},
for the electric field
\mathcal{E}
and the magnetic field
\mathcal{H}
. In detail, the displacement field
{\mathcal{D}}
in the time domain is

{\mathcal{D}}(x,t)=\perm_{0}\left(\mathcal{E}(x,t)+\int_{-\infty}^{t}\chi(x_{1},t-s)\mathcal{E}(x,s)\,{\rm d}s\right),

where
\chi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is called the response function or susceptibility. The above time-harmonic ansatz produces
{\mathcal{D}}(x,t)=D(x)e^{-{\rm i}\omega t}+\bar{D}(x)e^{{\rm i}\overline{\omega}t}
with

D=\perm_{0}(1+{\mathcal{F}}_{t}(\chi)(x_{1},\omega))E,

where
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
denotes the temporal Fourier transform, i.e.,

{\mathcal{F}}_{t}(\chi)(x_{1},\omega):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(x_{1},t)e^{{\rm i}\omega t}\,{\rm d}t.

Setting

\perm(x_{1},\omega):=\perm_{0}(1+{\mathcal{F}}_{t}(\chi)(x_{1},\omega)),

one recovers
D
in (
1.8
).

In the case of a non-dispersive dielectric in
x_{1}>0
and a dispersive metal in
x_{1}<0
the following is a simple example,

{\mathcal{F}}_{t}(\chi)(x_{1},\omega):=\begin{cases}{\mathcal{F}}_{t}(\chi_{m})(\omega)&\text{for }x_{1}<0,\\
\eta&\text{for }x_{1}>0,\end{cases}

(1.9)

where
\eta>0
and where physically relevant frequency dependent permittivities
\chi_{m}
are, e.g., the Drude model

{\mathcal{F}}_{t}(\chi_{m})(\omega)=-\frac{c_{D}}{\omega^{2}+{\rm i}\gamma\omega}

(1.10)

with
c_{D},\gamma>0
and the Lorentz model

{\mathcal{F}}_{t}(\chi_{m})(\omega)=-\frac{c_{L}}{\omega^{2}+{\rm i}\gamma\omega-\omega_{*}^{2}}

(1.11)

with
c_{L},\gamma,\omega_{*}>0
, see e.g.
[
5
,
10
,
23
]
.
For an inhomogeneous structure, e.g., a crystal, the constants in (
1.9
), (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) may be
x
-dependent.

In the second-order formulation for the
E
-field, system (
1.8
) translates to (
1.1
), which is our object of study. The geometry we are considering consists of the two half-spaces

\mathbb{R}^{N}_{\pm}:=\mathbb{R}_{\pm}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-1},N=2,3,\quad\text{where}\quad\mathbb{R}_{\pm}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\pm x>0\}.

In each half-space, the permittivity is smooth (that is,
W^{3,\infty}
) for
\omega
inside
D(\perm)
with
\perm
given by (
1.2
).

Throughout the paper the complex square root of
z=|z|e^{{\rm i}\theta},\theta\in(-\pi,\pi]
is defined as
\sqrt{z}:=\sqrt{|z|}e^{{\rm i}\theta/2}
. Hence
\mathop{\rm Re}(\sqrt{z})\geq 0
.

As mentioned above, we study the spectral problem corresponding to (
1.1
) as an operator pencil. We first define

{\mathcal{L}}:=(L(\omega))_{\omega\in D(\perm)},

(1.12)

L(\omega):T(\nabla)-B(\omega),\quad\text{where}\quad T(\nabla)u:=\nabla\times\nabla\times u,\ B(\omega)u:=\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)u.

(1.13)

Note that in
[
7
]
the pencil
{\mathcal{L}}
was defined using two parameters, namely
\omega
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, where
\lambda
was needed in order to define isolatedness of eigenvalues and hence the discrete spectrum. Here, however, we do not discuss these concepts and therefore omit the parameter
\lambda
.

Next, we describe our choice of function spaces. We work in the Hilbert space

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}:=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N},\mathbb{C}^{3})

and choose the domain

{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}:=\{E\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}:\nabla\times E,\ \nabla\times\nabla\times E\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3},\nabla\cdot(\perm(\cdot,\omega)E)=0\ (\text{distributionally})\}.

(1.14)

Due to the divergence condition in the domain and because the divergence of the curl vanishes,
L(\omega)
maps into the space of divergence free functions

L(\omega):{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}\to{\mathcal{R}}:=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}:\nabla\cdot f=0\}.

We are in a non-standard situation with the domain of the operator not being a subspace of the codomain.

Note that
{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
can be written using
L^{2}
-conditions on each half-space and jump conditions across the interface, namely,

{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}=\{E\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}:\nabla\times E,\ \nabla\times\nabla\times E\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm}^{N})^{3},\nabla\cdot(\perm(\cdot,\omega)E)=0\ \text{on }\mathbb{R}^{N}_{\pm}\ \text{ and \eqref{E:IFC} holds}\}

\displaystyle T_{+}^{n}(\perm(\cdot,\omega)E)-T_{-}^{n}(\perm(\cdot,\omega)E)=0,

(1.15)

\displaystyle T_{+}^{t}(E)-T_{-}^{t}(E)=0,

\displaystyle T_{+}^{t}(\nabla\times E)-T_{-}^{t}(\nabla\times E)=0,

where the normal trace operators
T_{\pm}^{n}
and the tangential trace operators
T_{\pm}^{t}
are defined in Appendix
B
.

Let us denote the variables for the directions parallel to the interface by
x_{\parallel}
, i.e.,

x_{\parallel}:=x_{2}\penalty\ {\rm if}\penalty\ N=2\quad{\rm and}\quad x_{\parallel}:=(x_{2},x_{3})^{\top}\penalty\ {\rm if}\penalty\ N=3.

To elements of
{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
we apply the Fourier transformation in the
x_{\parallel}-
variables, which in the special case of
E(x_{1},\cdot)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{N-1})
is given by the formula

u(x_{1},k):=\begin{cases}u(x_{1},k_{2}):=\widehat{E}(x_{1},k_{2}):=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}E(x_{1},x_{2})e^{-{\rm i}k_{2}x_{2}}\,{\rm d}x_{2},&N=2,\\
\\
u(x_{1},k_{2},k_{3}):=\widehat{E}(x_{1},k_{2},k_{3}):=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})e^{-{\rm i}(k_{2}x_{2}+k_{3}x_{3})}\,{\rm d}(x_{2},x_{3}),&N=3\end{cases}

with the variable
k\in\mathbb{R}^{N-1},
and otherwise is defined by the standard extension procedure.

The functions
\nabla\times E
and
\nabla\times\nabla\times E
transform to

\displaystyle\nabla_{k}\times u=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{\rm i}k_{2}u_{3}\\
-\partial_{x_{1}}u_{3}\\
\partial_{x_{1}}u_{2}-{\rm i}k_{2}u_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad T_{k}(\partial_{x_{1}})u:=\nabla_{k}\times\nabla_{k}\times u=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}k_{2}^{2}&{\rm i}k_{2}\partial_{x_{1}}&0\\
{\rm i}k_{2}\partial_{x_{1}}&-\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}&0\\
0&0&k_{2}^{2}-\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\qquad\text{if}\ N=2,

\displaystyle\nabla_{k}\times u=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{\rm i}k_{2}u_{3}-{\rm i}k_{3}u_{2}\\
{\rm i}k_{3}u_{1}-\partial_{x_{1}}u_{3}\\
\partial_{x_{1}}u_{2}-{\rm i}k_{2}u_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad T_{k}(\partial_{x_{1}})u:=\nabla_{k}\times\nabla_{k}\times u=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}|k|^{2}&{\rm i}k_{2}\partial_{x_{1}}&{\rm i}k_{3}\partial_{x_{1}}\\
{\rm i}k_{2}\partial_{x_{1}}&k_{3}^{2}-\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}&-k_{2}k_{3}\\
{\rm i}k_{3}\partial_{x_{1}}&-k_{2}k_{3}&k_{2}^{2}-\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\qquad\text{if}\ N=3,

with
|k|^{2}=k_{2}^{2}+k_{3}^{2}
and

\nabla_{k}:=\begin{cases}(\partial_{x_{1}},{\rm i}k_{2},0)^{\top},&N=2,\\
(\partial_{x_{1}},{\rm i}k_{2},{\rm i}k_{3})^{\top},&N=3.\end{cases}

This leads us to consider the operator
\widehat{L}
, where

\widehat{L}(\omega)u:=T_{k}(\partial_{x_{1}})u-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)u,\ x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},k\in\mathbb{R}^{N-1}.

(1.16)

Clearly, the operator
\widehat{L}(\omega)
for
N=2
can be obtained from
\widehat{L}(\omega)
for
N=3
by setting
k_{3}=0
. However, to avoid any confusion, we present many properties of
\widehat{L}
in the two cases separately.

The domain of
\widehat{L}(\omega)
can be rewritten via conditions on each component as

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}=

\displaystyle\left\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{3}:k_{2}u_{3},u_{3}^{\prime},{\rm i}k_{2}u_{1}-u_{2}^{\prime},k_{2}^{2}u_{1}+{\rm i}k_{2}u_{2}^{\prime},{\rm i}k_{2}u_{1}^{\prime}-u_{2}^{\prime\prime},-u_{3}^{\prime\prime}+k_{2}^{2}u_{3}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm}^{2}),\right.

(1.17)

\displaystyle\left.\text{ and \eqref{E:divergence-u}, \eqref{E:IFC-u} hold}\right\},\hbox{ for }N=2,

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}=

\displaystyle\left\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{3}:k_{2}u_{3}-k_{3}u_{2},{\rm i}k_{3}u_{1}-u_{3}^{\prime},{\rm i}k_{2}u_{1}-u_{2}^{\prime},|k|^{2}u_{1}+{\rm i}k_{2}u_{2}^{\prime}+{\rm i}k_{3}u_{3}^{\prime},\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip-28.45274pt{\rm i}k_{2}u_{1}^{\prime}-u_{2}^{\prime\prime}+k_{3}^{2}u_{2}-k_{2}k_{3}u_{3},{\rm i}k_{3}u_{1}^{\prime}-u_{3}^{\prime\prime}-k_{2}k_{3}u_{2}+k_{2}^{2}u_{3}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm}^{3}),\text{ and \eqref{E:divergence-u}, \eqref{E:IFC-u} hold}\right\},\hbox{ for }N=3,

where
f^{\prime}:=\partial_{x_{1}}f
, and

\displaystyle(\perm(\cdot,\omega)u_{1})^{\prime}+{\rm i}\perm(\cdot,\omega)k_{2}u_{2}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\pm}\quad\text{if}\quad N=2,

(1.18)

\displaystyle(\perm(\cdot,\omega)u_{1})^{\prime}+{\rm i}\perm(\cdot,\omega)(k_{2}u_{2}+k_{3}u_{3})

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}\quad\text{if}\quad N=3,

\displaystyle\llbracket\perm(\cdot,\omega)u_{1}\rrbracket

\displaystyle:=\widehat{T_{+}^{n}}(\perm(\cdot,\omega)u)-\widehat{T_{-}^{n}}(\perm(\cdot,\omega)u)=0,

(1.19)

\displaystyle(\llbracket u_{2}\rrbracket,\llbracket u_{3}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle:=\widehat{T_{+}^{t}}(u)-\widehat{T_{-}^{t}}(u)=0,

\displaystyle(\llbracket(\nabla_{k}\times u)_{2}\rrbracket,\llbracket(\nabla_{k}\times u)_{3}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle=\widehat{T_{+}^{t}}(\nabla_{k}\times u)-\widehat{T_{-}^{t}}(\nabla_{k}\times u)=0,

where the normal trace operators
\widehat{T_{\pm}^{n}}
and the tangential trace operators
\widehat{T_{\pm}^{t}}
in the Fourier space are defined in Appendix
B
. The definitions are such that
\widehat{T_{\pm}^{n}}{\mathcal{F}}_{t}={\mathcal{F}}_{t}T_{\pm}^{n}
in
\Hdiv(\mathbb{R}^{N}_{\pm})
and
\widehat{T_{\pm}^{t}}{\mathcal{F}}_{t}={\mathcal{F}}_{t}T_{\pm}^{t}
in
\Hcurl(\mathbb{R}^{N}_{\pm})
, see Appendix
B
.
In (
1.19
) and the rest of the paper we use the bracket notation both for jumps of normal and tangential traces.

The codomain
{\mathcal{R}}
in the Fourier variables reads

\widehat{{\mathcal{R}}}=\begin{cases}\{r\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{3}:r_{1}^{\prime}+{\rm i}k_{2}r_{2}=0\},&N=2,\\
\{r\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{3}:r_{1}^{\prime}+{\rm i}(k_{2}r_{2}+k_{3}r_{3})=0\},&N=3.\end{cases}

(1.20)

On
{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
we introduce the norm

\|u\|_{{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}}:=\sqrt{\langle u,u\rangle_{{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}}},\ \langle u,v\rangle_{{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}}:=\langle u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}+\langle T(\nabla)u,T(\nabla)v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})},

(1.21)

i.e., the graph norm corresponding to
T(\nabla)
.

Lemma 1.1
.

({\mathcal{D}}_{\omega},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}})
is a Hilbert space.

Proof.

Due to the integration by parts in Lemma
A.1

\|\nabla\times v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}}^{2}=\langle v,\nabla\times\nabla\times v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}}\leq\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}}\|\nabla\times\nabla\times v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}}

for each
v\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
and
N=2,3
. Hence the norm

\|v\|:=\left(\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}}^{2}+\|\nabla\times v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}}^{2}+\|\nabla\times\nabla\times v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}}
.

The rest of the proof is completely analogous to the proof of Lemma 4.2 in
[
7
]
.
∎

Corollary 1.2
.

The operator
T(\nabla):{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}\to{\mathcal{R}}
is closed.

Proof.

For a sequence
(u_{k},T(\nabla)u_{k})
with
u_{k}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
and
u_{k}\to u
and
T(\nabla)u_{k}\to v
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}
we need to show that
v=T(\nabla)u.
The convergence assumption implies that
(u_{k})
is Cauchy in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
.
Using Lemma
1.1
and the fact that the convergence in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
implies convergence in
L^{2}
, we get
u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
and
T(\nabla)u_{k}\to T(\nabla)u
. We conclude that
v=T(\nabla)u.

∎

Because of Corollary
1.2
for any fixed
\omega
and
\lambda
the operator
L(\omega;\lambda)
is a sum of a bounded and a closed operator, and thus closed. Hence, it makes sense to study its spectrum.

1.1.1
Definition of the Spectrum

Here we give the definitions of those spectral sets which are studied in the rest of the paper. For more details on the spectra of operator pencils, we refer to
[
7
]
.

The
resolvent set
of the pencil
{\mathcal{L}}
is defined as

\rho({\mathcal{L}}):=\{\omega\in D(\perm):L(\omega):{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}\to{\mathcal{R}}\text{ is bijective with a bounded inverse}\},

and the
spectrum
of
{\mathcal{L}}
is given by

\sigma({\mathcal{L}}):=D(\perm)\setminus\rho({\mathcal{L}})=\{\omega\in D(\perm):0\in\sigma(L(\omega))\},

(1.22)

where
\sigma(L(\omega))
is the spectrum (defined in the standard sense) of the operator
L(\omega)
at a
fixed
\omega
.

Next, the
point spectrum
is

\sigma_{p}({\mathcal{L}}):=\{\omega\in D(\perm):\exists u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}\setminus\{0\}:L(\omega)u=0\}.

Elements of
\sigma_{p}({\mathcal{L}})
are called
eigenvalues
of
{\mathcal{L}}
. The
geometric multiplicity
of an eigenvalue
\omega
is given by
\dim{\mathrm{ker\,}}(L(\omega))
.

A sequence
(u^{(n)})\subset{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
is called a
Weyl sequence at
\omega
if

\|u^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}}=1\ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},u^{(n)}\rightharpoonup 0\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3},\text{ and }\|L(\omega)u^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}}\to 0\ (n\to\infty).

The
Weyl spectrum
is

\sweyl({\mathcal{L}}):=\{\omega\in D(\perm):\text{ a Weyl sequence at $\omega$ exists}\}.

1.2
Main Results

Here we describe our main spectral results in a compact form. More details and all proofs will follow in subsequent sections.

Our results are subdivided into the case of general permittivity functions
\perm_{\pm}
satisfying just (
1.3
), the more special case where both
\perm_{+}(\cdot,\omega)
and
\perm_{-}(\cdot,\omega)
are periodic, and the even more special (but still physically very relevant) homogeneous case where
\perm_{+}
and
\perm_{-}
are independent of the spatial variable, i.e.,
\perm_{\pm}(x_{1},\omega)=\perm_{\pm}(\omega).
The results for the homogeneous case with
N=3
are a direct extension of
[
7
]
, where the same setting in dimensions
N=1,2
, was studied.

The results on general
\perm_{\pm}
contain various assumptions which are too lengthy to be mentioned here in this summary section. We refer to Theorem
3.2
, where a subset of the resolvent set is identified, to Theorems
4.6
and
4.13
which provide subsets of the Weyl spectrum corresponding to radiation in the
x_{1}
-direction (Theorem
4.6
) and in a direction along the interface
\{x_{1}=0\}
(Theorem
4.13
), and to Theorem
5.1
containing a partial result on the non-existence of eigenvalues.

For the periodic and the homogeneous case, the required assumptions can be stated much more concisely and hence we give more details here.

Theorem A

(periodic
\perm_{\pm}
)
.

Let
N=2,3
and suppose that in addition to (
1.3
) the functions
\perm_{\pm}(\cdot,\omega)
are
a_{\pm}
-periodic for each
\omega\in D(\perm)
.
Let
D_{\pm}^{(v)}(k)
and
D_{\pm}^{(w)}(k)
denote the discriminants of the differential equations (
2.15
) and (
2.16
), respectively, on the intervals
[0,a_{+}]
and
[-a_{-},0]
according to the index
\pm
, respectively (see Sec.
2.2
). Let
d(k)
and
\widetilde{d}(k)
be the quantities defined in Definition
3.1
for (
2.15
) and (
2.16
), respectively.

(a)

(
resolvent set
) Suppose that for some fixed
\omega\in D(\perm)\setminus\Omega
and all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{N-1}

D_{+}^{(v)}(k),D_{-}^{(v)}(k),D_{+}^{(w)}(k),D_{-}^{(w)}(k)\notin[-2,2],

d(k)\neq 0\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\widetilde{d}(k)\neq 0.

(1.23)

Furthermore, assume that

\perm_{+}(0,\omega)+\perm_{-}(0,\omega)\neq 0\quad\text{or}\quad\perm_{+}^{\prime}(0,\omega)-\perm_{-}^{\prime}(0,\omega)\neq 0.

(1.24)

Then
\omega\in\rho({\mathcal{L}})
.

(b)

(
radiation away from the interface
) For some fixed
\omega\in D(\perm)
suppose that
k_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N-1}
exists such that one of the following two options holds.

\displaystyle\mathrm{I)}\qquad\left(\omega\notin\Omega^{+}_{|k_{0}|^{2}}\ \text{and}\ D_{+}^{(v)}(k_{0})\in[-2,2]\right)\ \text{or}\ D_{+}^{(w)}(k_{0})\in[-2,2],

\displaystyle\mathrm{II)}\qquad\left(\omega\notin\Omega^{-}_{|k_{0}|^{2}}\ \text{and}\ D_{-}^{(v)}(k_{0})\in[-2,2]\right)\ \text{or}\ D_{-}^{(w)}(k_{0})\in[-2,2].

Then
\omega\in\sweyl({\mathcal{L}})
.

(c)

(
radiation along the interface
) For some fixed
\omega\in D(\perm)
suppose that
k_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N-1}
exists such that

\omega\notin\Omega^{+}_{|k_{0}|^{2}}\cup\Omega^{-}_{|k_{0}|^{2}}\penalty\ ,\quad D_{+}^{(v)}(k_{0}),D_{-}^{(v)}(k_{0})\notin[-2,2],\quad\text{and}\quad d(k)=0

or

D_{+}^{(w)}(k_{0}),D_{-}^{(w)}(k_{0})\notin[-2,2]\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{d}(k)=0.

Then
\omega\in\sweyl({\mathcal{L}})
.

(d)

(
eigenvalues
) There are no eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{L}}
of finite geometric multiplicity.

For the proof see Proposition
3.7
and Theorem
3.2
(for (a)), Lemma
4.7
(for (b)), Lemma
4.14
and Theorem
4.13
(for (c)), and Theorem
5.1
(for (d)).
We add that part (b) is proved by constructing Weyl sequences with their support moving to infinity in the
x_{1}
-direction (radiation in
x_{1}
), while for part (c) Weyl sequences localized at the interface
\{x_{1}=0\}
with their support moving to infinity in an
x_{\parallel}
-direction (radiation along the interface) are chosen.

For the radiation in
x_{1}
we study also the “asymptotically periodic” case where, for some
\omega\in D(\perm)
,

\perm_{\pm}(\cdot,\omega)=\perm_{p,\pm}(\cdot,\omega)+\perm_{as,\pm}(\cdot,\omega)

with
\perm_{p,\pm}(\cdot,\omega)
periodic and
\perm_{as,\pm}(\cdot,\omega)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm})
(for spectrum generated by equation (
2.16
)) or
\perm_{as,\pm}(\cdot,\omega)\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm})
(for spectrum generated by equation (
2.15
)). In Lemma
4.9
we prove that under the additional condition of stability, i.e., both solutions in the fundamental system of the corresponding periodic problem (with
\perm_{p}
instead of
\perm
) being bounded (on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
or
\mathbb{R}_{-}
) we get
\omega\in\sweyl({\mathcal{L}})
.

While Theorem
A
uses conditions which, in concrete examples, can be difficult to check explicitly, like the conditions on the discriminants, the next theorem on the homogeneous case is fully explicit. Recall that for
\perm_{\pm}(\cdot,\omega)=\perm_{\pm}(\omega)
the singular set
\Omega_{0}
is defined in (
1.4
).

Theorem B

(constant
\perm_{\pm}
)
.

Let
N=2,3
and suppose
\perm_{\pm}(\cdot,\omega)=\perm_{\pm}(\omega)\in\mathbb{C}
for each
\omega\in D(\perm)\setminus\Omega_{0}
. Define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{red}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\omega\in D(\perm)\setminus\Omega_{0}:\perm_{+}(\omega)+\perm_{-}(\omega)\neq 0,\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}^{2}(\omega)}{\perm_{+}(\omega)+\perm_{-}(\omega)},\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{-}^{2}(\omega)}{\perm_{+}(\omega)+\perm_{-}(\omega)}\notin[0,\infty),\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(\omega)\perm_{-}(\omega)}{\perm_{+}(\omega)+\perm_{-}(\omega)}\in(0,\infty)\right\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{red}}

\displaystyle:=\{\omega\in D(\perm)\setminus\Omega_{0}:\omega^{2}\perm_{\pm}(\omega)\in(0,\infty)\}.

Then

(a)

(
resolvent set
)

D(\perm)\setminus({\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{red}}\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{+}^{\mathrm{red}}\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{-}^{\mathrm{red}}\cup\Omega_{0})\subset\rho({\mathcal{L}}),

(b)

(
radiation orthogonal to the interface
)

{\mathcal{M}}_{+}^{\mathrm{red}}\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{-}^{\mathrm{red}}\subset\sweyl({\mathcal{L}}),

(c)

(
radiation along the interface
)

{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{red}}\subset\sweyl({\mathcal{L}}),

(d)

(
eigenvalues
)

\sigma_{p}({\mathcal{L}})\setminus\Omega_{0}=\emptyset.

The proof is given in Proposition
3.3
(for (a)), Lemma
4.1
(for (b)), Lemma
4.15
(for (c)), and Lemma
5.2
(for (d)).

Note that the index “red” in the notation of the above sets describes the fact that theses sets are “reduced” by
\Omega_{0}
being excluded.
It is interesting to note that for the case of homogeneous media in
\mathbb{R}^{N}_{\pm}
our results provide a full description of the spectrum outside the exceptional set
\Omega_{0}
, like in the 1D and 2D cases in
[
7
]
.
Indeed, in the homogeneous case, Theorem
B
shows that the set

{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{red}}\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{+}^{\mathrm{red}}\cup{\mathcal{M}}_{-}^{\mathrm{red}}
comprises the Weyl spectrum (outside
\Omega_{0}
), while the complement of this set is the resolvent set (outside
\Omega_{0}
). In addition, we know that there are no eigenvalues outside
\Omega_{0}
. This is in contrast with the case of
x_{1}
-dependent
\perm_{\pm}
, where
there is a potentially larger set in
D(\perm)
, for which we cannot decide whether points lie in the spectrum or the resolvent set.

Remark 1.3
.

Theorem
B
for the interface between two homogeneous media gives the same spectrum (outside
\Omega_{0}
) of the
N
-dimensional Maxwell operator as Theorem 3 in
[
7
]
for the two dimensional setting. Indeed, the sets
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{red}}
coincide with the sets
M_{\pm}
in Theorem 3 of
[
7
]
and, as we show next, the set

{\mathcal{N}}:=\left\{\omega\in D(\perm)\setminus\Omega_{0}:\exists a\geq 0\text{ such that }\omega^{2}\perm_{\pm}(\omega)\notin[a,\infty),\ \frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(\omega)\perm_{-}(\omega)}{\perm_{+}(\omega)+\perm_{-}(\omega)}=a\right\}

used in
[
7
]
equals
{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{red}}
.

For the proof of
{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{red}}={\mathcal{N}}
, let us label the three conditions

\perm_{+}+\perm_{-}\neq 0,\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{\pm}^{2}}{\perm_{+}+\perm_{-}}\notin[0,\infty),
and
\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}\perm_{-}}{\perm_{+}+\perm_{-}}\in(0,\infty)
in
{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{red}}
by (i), (ii), and (iii) respectively.
The two conditions
\omega^{2}\perm_{\pm}\notin[a,\infty)
and
\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}\perm_{-}}{\perm_{+}+\perm_{-}}=a
in
{\mathcal{N}}
will be labeled by (iv) and (v) respectively.

We first show
{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{red}}\subset{\mathcal{N}}.
If
\omega\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{red}}
, then (iii) implies
\omega^{2}\perm_{+}\perm_{-}=a(\perm_{+}+\perm_{-})
with some
a>0
and hence (v) holds. Also, dividing this equation by
\omega^{2}\perm_{-}^{2}
and using (ii) yields

\frac{\perm_{+}}{\perm_{-}}=a\frac{\perm_{+}+\perm_{-}}{\omega^{2}\perm_{-}^{2}}\notin[0,\infty).

(1.25)

Equation (
1.25
) can also be reformulated as

\omega^{2}\perm_{+}=a(1+\tfrac{\perm_{+}}{\perm_{-}})

and

\omega^{2}\perm_{-}=a(1+\tfrac{\perm_{-}}{\perm_{+}}).

Using the fact that
\tfrac{\perm_{+}}{\perm_{-}}\notin[0,\infty)
, we get
\omega^{2}\perm_{\pm}\notin[a,\infty)
, i.e., (iv).

Now let
\omega\in{\mathcal{N}}
. Property (v) and
\omega\notin\Omega_{0}
imply
\perm_{+}+\perm_{-}\neq 0
and
a>0
in (v) such that (i) and (iii) hold. To show (ii), first note that (v) can be rewritten as
\omega^{2}\perm_{+}=a(1+\tfrac{\perm_{+}}{\perm_{-}})
, which lies outside
[a,\infty)
due to (iv). Equivalently

\tfrac{\perm_{+}}{\perm_{-}}\notin[0,\infty)
. Finally, multiplying (v) by
\tfrac{\perm_{+}}{\perm_{-}}
, we obtain

\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}^{2}}{\perm_{+}+\perm_{-}}=a\frac{\perm_{+}}{\perm_{-}}\notin[0,\infty)

and similarly,
\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{-}^{2}}{\perm_{+}+\perm_{-}}\notin[0,\infty)
. In other words, (ii) holds and we conclude
\omega\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{red}}
.

2
Preliminaries

Before starting with the spectral considerations, we transform the problem to one that is essentially diagonal (in the new variables
v,w
), introduce some standard notation and prove important estimates for the periodic problem. The periodic setting provides an important application of our results.

2.1
Transformation of the Problem

The next lemma suggests that the problem

\widehat{L}(\omega)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\\
u_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}r_{1}\\
r_{2}\\
r_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\quad(x,k)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-1}

(2.1)

in
\widehat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}
for arbitrary
r\in\widehat{{\mathcal{R}}}
can be decoupled into two independent problems in the new variables
u_{1},v,w
. In fact, in the case
N=2
the problem is decoupled in the original variables due to the block diagonal structure of
\widehat{L}
. Nevertheless, in order to be able to work with the two cases
N=2
and
N=3
simultaneously, we use the new variables
u_{1},v,w
also for
N=2
.
Note that from (
2.4
), it is easy to see that
u_{1}
can be expressed in terms of
v
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
N=2,3
and
\omega\in D(\perm)
. Consider the operator
{\mathcal{U}}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}
given by

\left({\mathcal{U}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\\
u_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)(x_{1},k)=U(k)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\\
u_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(x_{1},k)\hbox{ for }k\in\mathbb{R}^{N-1}\setminus\{0\},

where

U(k)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&\mbox{\rm sign}(k)&0\\
0&0&\mbox{\rm sign}(k)\end{array}\right)\penalty\ {\rm with}\penalty\ k=k_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\penalty\ {\rm for}\penalty\ N=2,

U(k)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&\frac{k_{2}}{|k|}&\frac{k_{3}}{|k|}\\
0&-\frac{k_{3}}{|k|}&\frac{k_{2}}{|k|}\end{array}\right)\penalty\ {\rm with}\penalty\ k=(k_{2},k_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}\penalty\ {\rm for}\penalty\ N=3,

and
where
|k|=|k_{2}|
for
N=2
and
|k|=\sqrt{k_{2}^{2}+k_{3}^{2}}
for
N=3
.
Then
{\mathcal{U}}
is unitary and
{\mathcal{U}}:\widehat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}\to\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}
is bijective, where

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}:=

\displaystyle\left\{(u_{1},v,w)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{3}:v^{\prime}-{\rm i}|k|u_{1},v^{\prime\prime}-{\rm i}|k|u_{1}^{\prime},{\rm i}|k|v^{\prime}+|k|^{2}u_{1},w^{\prime},|k|w,w^{\prime\prime}-|k|^{2}w\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm}^{N}),\right.

\displaystyle\left.\text{and}\ \eqref{E:divergence-uvw}\ \text{and}\ \eqref{E:IFC-uvw}\ \text{hold}\right\}.

(\perm(\cdot,\omega)u_{1})^{\prime}+{\rm i}|k|\perm(\cdot,\omega)v=0\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{N}_{\pm},

(2.2)

\displaystyle\llbracket\perm(\cdot,\omega)u_{1}\rrbracket=\llbracket v\rrbracket=\llbracket v^{\prime}-{\rm i}|k|u_{1}\rrbracket=\llbracket w\rrbracket=\llbracket w^{\prime}\rrbracket=0.

(2.3)

The operator

\widetilde{L}(\omega)=\begin{pmatrix}|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)&{\rm i}|k|\partial_{x_{1}}&0\\
{\rm i}|k|\partial_{x_{1}}&-\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)&0\\
0&0&-\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}+|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)\end{pmatrix}

(2.4)

with the domain
\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}
satisfies

\widetilde{L}={\mathcal{U}}\widehat{L}{\mathcal{U}}^{*}
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma in detail for
N=3
. The case
N=2
follows analogously.

For fixed
k\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
, it is clear that the matrix
U(k)
is unitary. Therefore, the matrix operator
{\mathcal{U}}
, having only multiplication operators as entries, is unitary on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{3}
.

Let
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\widehat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}
, and set

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{1}\\
v\\
w\end{smallmatrix}\right)={\mathcal{U}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\\
u_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

(2.5)

Then

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}(\omega){\mathcal{U}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\\
u_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\left(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)\right)u_{1}+{\rm i}|k|v^{\prime}\\
{\rm i}|k|u_{1}^{\prime}-v^{\prime\prime}-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)v\\
-w^{\prime\prime}+\left(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)\right)w\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\left(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)\right)u_{1}+{\rm i}k_{2}u_{2}^{\prime}+{\rm i}k_{3}u_{3}^{\prime}\\
{\rm i}|k|u_{1}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{|k|}\left[k_{2}u_{2}^{\prime\prime}+k_{3}u_{3}^{\prime\prime}+\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)(k_{2}u_{2}+k_{3}u_{3})\right]\\
-\frac{1}{|k|}\left[-k_{3}u_{2}^{\prime\prime}+k_{2}u_{3}^{\prime\prime}-\left(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)\right)(-k_{3}u_{2}+k_{2}u_{3})\right]\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&\frac{k_{2}}{|k|}&\frac{k_{3}}{|k|}\\
0&-\frac{k_{3}}{|k|}&\frac{k_{2}}{|k|}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)&{\rm i}k_{2}\partial_{x_{1}}&{\rm i}k_{3}\partial_{x_{1}}\\
{\rm i}k_{2}\partial_{x_{1}}&k_{3}^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)-\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}&-k_{2}k_{3}\\
{\rm i}k_{3}\partial_{x_{1}}&-k_{2}k_{3}&k_{2}^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm(x_{1},\omega)-\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\\
u_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}\widehat{L}(\omega)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\\
u_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right),

so formally
\widetilde{L}{\mathcal{U}}={\mathcal{U}}\widehat{L}
, as claimed. To prove the full operator identity, it remains to check that
{\mathcal{U}}:\widehat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}\to\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}
is bijective.

Let
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\widehat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}
. The property
(\perm u_{1})^{\prime}+{\rm i}\perm(k_{2}u_{2}+k_{3}u_{3})=0
contained in
\widehat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}
gives, using (
2.5
),

(\perm u_{1})^{\prime}+{\rm i}|k|\perm v=0\penalty\ {\rm on}\ \mathbb{R}_{\pm}.

(2.7)

The conditions
u_{2},u_{3}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm}^{3}),\llbracket u_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket u_{3}\rrbracket=0
imply, using (
2.5
) and the boundedness of
\frac{k_{2}}{|k|}
and
\frac{k_{3}}{|k|}
,

v,w\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm}^{3}),\llbracket v\rrbracket=\llbracket w\rrbracket=0.

(2.8)

In the same way, the conditions
{\rm i}k_{2}u_{1}-u_{2}^{\prime}
,
{\rm i}k_{3}u_{1}-u_{3}^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm}^{3}),\llbracket{\rm i}k_{2}u_{1}-u_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\rm i}k_{3}u_{1}-u_{3}^{\prime}\rrbracket=0

amount to

{\rm i}|k|u_{1}-v^{\prime},w^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}),\llbracket{\rm i}|k|u_{1}-v^{\prime}\rrbracket=\llbracket w^{\prime}\rrbracket=0,

(2.9)

and
{\rm i}k_{2}u_{1}^{\prime}-u_{2}^{\prime\prime}+k_{3}^{2}u_{2}-k_{2}k_{3}u_{3},{\rm i}k_{3}u_{1}^{\prime}-u_{3}^{\prime\prime}-k_{2}k_{3}u_{2}+k_{2}^{2}u_{3}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm})
to

{\rm i}|k|u_{1}^{\prime}-v^{\prime\prime},-w^{\prime\prime}+|k|^{2}w\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}).

(2.10)

The remaining conditions
k_{2}u_{3}-k_{3}u_{2},|k|^{2}u_{1}+{\rm i}k_{2}u_{2}^{\prime}+{\rm i}k_{3}u_{3}^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm})
give

|k|w,|k|^{2}u_{1}+{\rm i}|k|v^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}).

(2.11)

The conditions
u_{1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}),\llbracket\perm u_{1}\rrbracket=0
remain unchanged. (
2.7
) - (
2.11
) imply
(u_{1},v,w)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
.

Conversely, let
(u_{1},v,w)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
. Hence (
2.7
) - (
2.11
) are true. We define
u_{2},u_{3}
by inversion of (
2.5
), i.e.

{u_{2}\choose u_{3}}:=\frac{1}{|k|}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}k_{2}&-k_{3}\\
k_{3}&k_{2}\end{array}\right){v\choose w}.

(2.12)

Hence (
2.8
) implies

\displaystyle u_{2},u_{3}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}),\llbracket u_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket u_{3}\rrbracket=0.

By (
2.11
),

\displaystyle k_{2}u_{3}-k_{3}u_{2}=|k|w\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}),

and by (
2.9
)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm i}k_{2}u_{1}-u_{2}^{\prime}\\
{\rm i}k_{3}u_{1}-u_{3}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{|k|}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}k_{2}&-k_{3}\\
k_{3}&k_{2}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm i}|k|u_{1}-v^{\prime}\\
-w^{\prime}\end{array}\right)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm})^{2},

and also
\llbracket{\rm i}k_{2}u_{1}-u_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\rm i}k_{3}u_{1}-u_{3}^{\prime}\rrbracket=0
.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm i}k_{2}u_{1}^{\prime}-u_{2}^{\prime\prime}+k_{3}^{2}u_{2}-k_{2}k_{3}u_{3}\\
{\rm i}k_{3}u_{1}^{\prime}-u_{3}^{\prime\prime}-k_{2}k_{3}u_{2}+k_{2}^{2}u_{3}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{|k|}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}k_{2}&-k_{3}\\
k_{3}&k_{2}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm i}|k|u_{1}^{\prime}-v^{\prime\prime}\\
-w^{\prime\prime}+|k|^{2}w\end{array}\right)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm})^{2}

by (
2.10
). Furthermore,

\displaystyle|k|^{2}u_{1}+{\rm i}k_{2}u_{2}^{\prime}+{\rm i}k_{3}u_{3}^{\prime}=|k|^{2}u_{1}+{\rm i}|k|v^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm})

by (
2.11
). Finally, (
2.7
) implies

\displaystyle(\perm u_{1})^{\prime}+{\rm i}\perm(k_{2}u_{2}+k_{3}u_{3})=(\perm u_{1})^{\prime}+{\rm i}|k|\perm v=0{\rm\penalty\ on\penalty\ }\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}.

Altogether,
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\widehat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Next, we reformulate the transformed equations
\widetilde{L}(u_{1},v,w)^{\top}={\mathcal{U}}r

so that both
v
and
w
satisfy a Sturm-Liouville type equation. This turns out to be useful in the later analysis.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose that
\omega\in D(\perm)\setminus\Omega
. Problem (
2.1
) for
u\in\widehat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}
is equivalent to the following “almost” decoupled system for

(u_{1},v,w)^{\top}={\mathcal{U}}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})^{\top}\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}
given by

\begin{array}[]{D{r} D{l} D{l}}-\left(\frac{\perm}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm}v^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}+\perm v&=\frac{{\rm i}|k|\perm^{\prime}}{(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm)^{2}}r_{1}+\frac{\mbox{\rm sign}(k_{2})\perm}{(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm)}r_{2}&{\rm if}\penalty\ N=2,\\
-\left(\frac{\perm}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm}v^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}+\perm v&=\frac{{\rm i}|k|\perm^{\prime}}{(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm)^{2}}r_{1}+\frac{\perm}{|k|(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm)}(k_{2}r_{2}+k_{3}r_{3})&{\rm if}\penalty\ N=3,\end{array}

(2.15)

\begin{array}[]{D{r} D{l} D{l}}\\
-w^{\prime\prime}+(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm)w&=\mbox{\rm sign}(k_{2})r_{3}&{\rm if}\penalty\ N=2,\\
-w^{\prime\prime}+(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm)w&=\frac{1}{|k|}(k_{2}r_{3}-k_{3}r_{2})&{\rm if}\penalty\ N=3,\end{array}

(2.16)

u_{1}=-\frac{{\rm i}|k|}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm}v^{\prime}+\frac{1}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm}r_{1}.

(2.17)

Proof.

The proof is carried out for the three dimensional setting. The case
N=2
is obtained from this proof by simply setting
k_{3}=0
and replacing
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\widehat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\omega}
denote a solution of (
2.1
).
The first equation in (
2.1
) implies

u_{1}=\frac{r_{1}-{\rm i}k_{2}u_{2}^{\prime}-{\rm i}k_{3}u_{3}^{\prime}}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm}=\frac{r_{1}-{\rm i}|k|v^{\prime}}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm},

i.e. (
2.17
), and

u_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{r_{1}^{\prime}-{\rm i}|k|v^{\prime\prime}}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm}+\frac{\omega^{2}\perm^{\prime}}{(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm)^{2}}(r_{1}-{\rm i}|k|v^{\prime}).

(2.18)

Using Lemma
2.1
, the second and third equation in (
2.1
) give

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\rm i}|k|u_{1}^{\prime}-(v^{\prime\prime}+\omega^{2}\perm v)=\frac{1}{|k|}(k_{2}r_{2}+k_{3}r_{3}),\\
-w^{\prime\prime}+(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm)w=\frac{1}{|k|}(k_{2}r_{3}-k_{3}r_{2}).\end{split}

(2.19)

The second equation in (
2.19
) is just (
2.16
). Inserting (
2.18
) into the first equation gives

\displaystyle\frac{\omega^{2}}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm}\left\{\perm v^{\prime\prime}+\frac{|k|^{2}\perm^{\prime}}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm}v^{\prime}-\perm(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm)v\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{{\rm i}|k|}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm}r_{1}^{\prime}-\frac{{\rm i}|k|\omega^{2}\perm^{\prime}}{(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm)^{2}}r_{1}+\frac{1}{|k|}(k_{2}r_{2}+k_{3}r_{3}).

(2.20)

Using (
1.20
), we find equation (
2.15
).

Conversely, let
(u_{1},v,w)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
satisfy (
2.15
), (
2.16
), (
2.17
). By Lemma
2.1
, equation (
2.1
) is equivalent to

\widetilde{L}(\omega)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u_{1}\\
v\\
w\end{smallmatrix}\right)={\mathcal{U}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}r_{1}\\
r_{2}\\
r_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}r_{1}\\
\frac{1}{|k|}(k_{2}r_{2}+k_{3}r_{3})\\
\frac{1}{|k|}(-k_{3}r_{2}+k_{2}r_{3})\end{smallmatrix}\right).

(2.21)

The third equation in (
2.21
) is simply (
2.16
). From (
2.17
), we deduce
(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm)u_{1}=-{\rm i}|k|v^{\prime}+r_{1}
, giving the first equation in (
2.21
).
Differentiating (
2.17
), we can replace
u_{1}^{\prime}
in the second equation of (
2.21
). Again using (
1.20
), we recover the first equation in (
2.19
). This is precisely the second equation in (
2.21
).
∎

In a further transformation, we will now rewrite the expression involving
v
in equation (
2.15
). This will be useful for later calculations, as it will allow us to treat the
v
- and
w
-components simultaneously. However, we caution that this is only a transformation of the differential equation, and we will not consider how the domain of
\widetilde{L}(\omega)
is transformed.

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose that
\omega\in D(\perm)\setminus\Omega
.
On
\mathbb{R}_{-}\cup\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the equation

-\left(\frac{\perm}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm}v^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}+\perm v=0

(2.22)

can be rewritten as

-z^{\prime\prime}+\left(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm+\frac{3\perm^{\prime 2}}{4\perm^{2}}-\frac{\perm^{\prime\prime}}{2\perm}\right)z=0,

(2.23)

where
z=\frac{\sqrt{\omega^{2}\perm}}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm}v^{\prime}
. Moreover, solutions
v
of (
2.22
) can be recovered from solutions
z
of (
2.23
) via

v=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega^{2}\perm}}z^{\prime}+\frac{\perm^{\prime}}{2\perm\sqrt{\omega^{2}\perm}}z.

(2.24)

Remark 2.4
.

We note that in general, for
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
,
\sqrt{z_{1}z_{2}}\neq\sqrt{z_{1}}\sqrt{z_{2}}
. However, for a complex-valued function
f
, we still have

\sqrt{f}\ ^{\prime}=\frac{f^{\prime}}{2\sqrt{f}}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{f}}\right)^{\prime}=-\frac{f^{\prime}}{2f\sqrt{f}}.

(2.25)

This can be seen by differentiating
\left(\sqrt{f}\right)^{2}=f
and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{f}}\sqrt{f}=1
, respectively.

Proof.

To shorten the notation we define

W_{\pm}:=\omega^{2}\perm_{\pm}.

For a solution
v
of (
2.22
),
let

y=\frac{W_{\pm}}{|k|^{2}-W_{\pm}}\,v^{\prime}
.
Then
y^{\prime}=W_{\pm}v
and

y^{\prime\prime}=W_{\pm}^{\prime}\,v+W_{\pm}\,v^{\prime}=\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime}}{W_{\pm}}\,y^{\prime}+(|k|^{2}-W_{\pm})\,y.

(2.26)

Due to the definition of
z
, we have
y=\sqrt{W_{\pm}}\,z
, and (using Remark
2.4
)

\displaystyle y^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime}}{2\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}\,z+\sqrt{W_{\pm}}\,z^{\prime},

\displaystyle y^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime\prime}}{2\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}-\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime 2}}{4W_{\pm}\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}\right)z+\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}\,z^{\prime}+\sqrt{W_{\pm}}\,z^{\prime\prime}

and from equation
(\ref{eq:yeq})
also

y^{\prime\prime}=\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime}}{W_{\pm}}\left(\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime}}{2\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}\,z+\sqrt{W_{\pm}}\,z^{\prime}\right)+(|k|^{2}-W_{\pm})\,\sqrt{W_{\pm}}\,z.

Combining the last two equations gives

-z^{\prime\prime}+\left(|k|^{2}-W_{\pm}+\frac{3W_{\pm}^{\prime 2}}{4W_{\pm}^{2}}-\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime\prime}}{2W_{\pm}}\right)z=0.

This is (
2.23
).

Conversely, for a solution
z
of (
2.23
), let
v
be defined by (
2.24
). We calculate

\displaystyle v^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}z^{\prime\prime}+\left(\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime}}{2W_{\pm}\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}\right)^{\prime}\,z

(2.27)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}\left[|k|^{2}-W_{\pm}+\frac{3(W_{\pm}^{\prime})^{2}}{4W_{\pm}^{2}}-\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime\prime}}{2W_{\pm}}\right]z+\left[\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime\prime}}{2W_{\pm}\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}-\frac{3(W_{\pm}^{\prime})^{2}}{4W_{\pm}^{2}\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}\right]z

(2.28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{|k|^{2}-W_{\pm}}{\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}\,z,

(2.29)

which gives

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\perm_{\pm}}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{\pm}}v^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\perm_{\pm}}{\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}z\right)^{\prime}\ =\ \frac{1}{\omega^{2}}\left(\sqrt{W_{\pm}}z\right)^{\prime}\ =\ \frac{1}{\omega^{2}}\left(\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime}}{2\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}z+\sqrt{W_{\pm}}z^{\prime}\right)

(2.30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{W_{\pm}}{\omega^{2}}\left(\frac{W_{\pm}^{\prime}}{2W_{\pm}\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}z+\frac{1}{\sqrt{W_{\pm}}}z^{\prime}\right)\ =\ \perm_{\pm}v,

(2.31)

i.e.,
v
solves (
2.22
).
∎

2.2
Notation and Fundamental Estimates for Periodic Media

For the case of periodic
\perm_{\pm}(\cdot,\omega)
we consider a periodic
\perm_{p}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
, which is the periodic extension of a periodic
\perm_{+}(\cdot,\omega)
or
\perm_{-}(\cdot,\omega)
to
\mathbb{R}
. Then many of the spectral results and related estimates can be provided in a more explicit form. Hence, we study the periodic case as an important example.

As we have shown in Lemma
2.2
and Lemma
2.3
, the Maxwell spectral problem can be transformed and decoupled into spectral problems for Sturm-Liouville or Schrödinger operators. In this section we provide some well-known general results from the Floquet theory of
Sturm-Liouville equations with periodic coefficients (see also

[
19
,
13
,
6
]
) and some fundamental estimates in the
case of a Schrödinger-type equation which may be of more general interest.
These estimates do not seem to appear in the literature.

Equations (
2.15
) and (
2.16
) with
r=0
are of the Sturm-Liouville type

-(Py^{\prime})^{\prime}+Qy=0.

(2.32)

In the case of (
2.15
) we have

P=P_{v}:=\frac{\perm_{p}}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{p}},\quad Q=Q_{v}:=\perm_{p}

(2.33)

and in the case of (
2.16
) we have

P=P_{w}:=1,\quad Q=Q_{w}:=|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{p}.

(2.34)

Equation (
2.32
) can be rewritten as a first-order system for the vector-valued function

(y,Py^{\prime})^{\top}
.
The canonical fundamental system of this system is the
2\times 2
matrix-valued solution of the initial value problem

\Phi^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{1}{P}\\
Q&0\end{pmatrix}\Phi,\qquad\Phi(0)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

When the coefficients of the equation have period
a>0
, the growth
of solutions of the equation can be characterised in terms of
the monodromy matrix
\Phi(a)
. This matrix has (Wronskian) determinant 1 and
therefore its eigenvalues are determined by its trace, i.e., the

discriminant

D:=\mathop{\rm Tr}\Phi(a)
.
If
D\notin\{-2,2\}
, then
\Phi(a)
has two distinct eigenvalues

e^{\kappa a}
and
e^{-\kappa a}
, where
\kappa\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
has

\mathop{\rm Re}\kappa\geq 0
and,
without loss of generality,
\mathop{\rm Im}\kappa\in(-\pi/a,\pi/a]
.
If
\Psi^{(1)},\Psi^{(2)}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
are corresponding eigenvectors, then
equation (
2.32
) has the linearly independent solutions
(Floquet solutions)

\psi_{1}:=(\Phi\Psi^{(1)})_{1}
and
\psi_{2}:=(\Phi\Psi^{(2)})_{1}
,
which can also be written in the form

\psi_{1}(x)=e^{\kappa x}\,p_{1}(x),\qquad\psi_{2}(x)=e^{-\kappa x}\,p_{2}(x)\qquad(x\in\mathbb{R})

(2.35)

with
a
-periodic functions
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
.
Like the eigenvectors, the functions
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are only fixed up to a multiplicative complex constant.

If
D\in(-2,2)
, then
\kappa
is purely imaginary and the Floquet solutions
(and consequently all solutions) are globally bounded; this is the case of

stability
.
If
D\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[-2,2]
, then
\mathop{\rm Re}\kappa>0
and
\psi_{1}
is
exponentially small at
-\infty
but unbounded at
\infty
,
\psi_{2}
is
unbounded at
-\infty
and exponentially small at
\infty
, so all non-trivial solutions
of (
2.32
) on
\mathbb{R}
are unbounded; this is the case of
instability
.
If
D\in\{-2,2\}
, then
\Phi(a)
only has one eigenvalue, either 1 or -1. If it has
geometric multiplicity 2,
then
\Phi(a)
is the unit matrix or its negative and all solutions of
(
2.32
) are
2a
-periodic and globally bounded (stability).
If the eigenvalue has geometric multiplicity 1, then (
2.32
) has one
bounded solution (up to multiples) while all other solutions are unbounded;
this is the case of
conditional stability
.

We now give some fundamental estimates for the Schrödinger-type equation,
i.e., (
2.32
) with
P\equiv 1
. Note that, with
r=0
, equation (
2.16
)
is of this form; we have seen in Lemma
2.3
that equation (
2.15
) can also be transformed
into this type.
In this spirit, consider the differential equation

-u^{\prime\prime}+(l^{2}-V)\,u=0,

(2.36)

where
l\geq 0
and

V:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is bounded and periodic of period
a>0
.
Then
\kappa
and the periodic functions
p_{1},p_{2}
in equation (
2.35
)
depend on
l
. In the following, we give uniform bounds on
\kappa
, and on
p_{1},p_{2}
and their
derivatives for suitable
l
-dependent choice of the multiplicative constants
for these functions and for sufficiently large
l
.

Lemma 2.5
.

With
\kappa
defined as above for equation (
2.36
),
there exist
C>0
and
l_{0}>0

such that

|\kappa(l)-l|\leq\frac{C}{l}\qquad(l\geq l_{0}).

(2.37)

Proof.

Consider the solution
u
of equation (
2.36
) with initial values

u(0)=1
,
u^{\prime}(0)=l
, and set

w(x)=e^{-lx}\,u(x)-1

(x\in[0,a])
. Then

w(0)=w^{\prime}(0)=0
and
w
satisfies the differential equation

w^{\prime\prime}+2lw^{\prime}=-V\,(1+w).

(2.38)

By the variation of constants formula, we obtain

\left(\begin{matrix}w(x)\\
w^{\prime}(x)\end{matrix}\right)=\frac{1}{2l}\int_{0}^{x}V(t)\,(1+w(t))\left(\begin{matrix}-1+e^{-2l(x-t)}\\
-2le^{-2l(x-t)}\end{matrix}\right)dt,

(2.39)

which implies

|w(x)|\leq\frac{1}{2l}\,\|V\|_{\infty}\,(1+\|w\|_{\infty})\int_{0}^{x}(1-e^{-2l(x-t)})\,dt\qquad(x\in[0,a])

and hence, with
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}:=\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}([0,a])}
,

\|w\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2l}\,\|V\|_{\infty}\,(1+\|w\|_{\infty})\,a.

Therefore we find for all
l>\|V\|_{\infty}a
the estimate

\|w\|_{\infty}\leq\left(1-\frac{a}{2l}\|V\|_{\infty}\right)^{-1}\frac{a}{2l}\|V\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{a}{l}\,\|V\|_{\infty}<1.

(2.40)

Furthermore, equation (
2.39
) also gives for all
l>\|V\|_{\infty}a

the estimate

\displaystyle\|w^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|V\|_{\infty}\,(1+\|w\|_{\infty})\sup_{x\in(0,a)}\int_{0}^{x}e^{-2l(x-t)}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\|V\|_{\infty}\,(1+1)\,\frac{1}{2l}\,(1-e^{-2la})\leq\frac{1}{l}\,\|V\|_{\infty}.

(2.41)

We now use d’Alembert’s formula to find a second solution of equation (
2.36
) that is linearly independent of
u
.
Note that for
l>\|V\|_{\infty}a
, the solution
u(x)=e^{lx}(1+w(x))

(x\in[0,a])
has no zeros due to the bound (
2.40
), so

\phi_{2}(x):=u(x)\int_{0}^{x}\frac{1}{u(t)^{2}}\,dt\qquad(x\in[0,a])

defines such a second solution. It has derivative

\phi_{2}^{\prime}(x)=u^{\prime}(x)\int_{0}^{x}\frac{1}{u(t)^{2}}\,dt+\frac{1}{u(x)}\qquad(x\in[0,a])

(2.42)

and hence initial values

\phi_{2}(0)=0,

\phi_{2}^{\prime}(0)=1.

Setting

\phi_{1}:=u-l\,\phi_{2}
,
we then see that

\Phi=\left(\begin{matrix}\phi_{1}&\phi_{2}\\
\phi_{1}^{\prime}&\phi_{2}^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right)

is the canonical fundamental system of equation (
2.36
), as

\Phi(0)=\mathbb{I}
.

We now consider
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}^{\prime}
in order to obtain an estimate for
the discriminant
D=\tr\Phi(a)
.
In the following, let
l_{0}>\|V\|_{\infty}\,\max\{1,a\}
.
Note that
\phi_{1}
can be written as

\phi_{1}(x)=e^{lx}\,(1+w(x))\,\left(1-l\int_{0}^{x}\frac{e^{-2lt}}{(1+w(t))^{2}}\,dt\right)\qquad(x\in[0,a]).

Since, for
l\geq l_{0}
,

\left|1-\frac{1}{(1+w(t))^{2}}\right|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}

by the estimate (
2.40
) (where the constant is independent of
l
),
we find

1-l\int_{0}^{x}\frac{e^{-2lt}}{(1+w(t))^{2}}\,dt=1-l\int_{0}^{x}e^{-2lt}\,dt+R_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\,(1+e^{-2lx})+R_{1}

with remainder term satisfying

|R_{1}|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}\,\frac{1}{2}\,(1+e^{-2lx})
.
Hence, using again the estimate (
2.40
),

\phi_{1}(x)=(\cosh lx+e^{lx}\,R_{1})(1+w(x))=\cosh lx+R_{2}\qquad(x\in[0,a])

with remainder term
|R_{2}|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}\,\cosh lx
.
Furthermore, by equation (
2.42
),

\displaystyle\phi_{2}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=e^{lx}\left(l\,(1+w(x))+w^{\prime}(x)\right)\int_{0}^{x}\frac{e^{-2lt}}{(1+w(t))^{2}}\,dt+\frac{e^{-lx}}{1+w(x)}

\displaystyle=(1+w(x)+\frac{1}{l}\,w^{\prime}(x))(\sinh lx+l\,e^{lx}\,R_{3})+e^{-lx}+R_{4}

\displaystyle=\sinh lx+e^{-lx}+R_{5}=\cosh lx+R_{5}\qquad(x\in[0,a])

for
l\geq l_{0}
, where

|R_{3}|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}\,\frac{1}{2l}\,(1-e^{-2lx})
,

|R_{4}|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}\,e^{-lx}
by (
2.40
).
Again using (
2.40
) and (
2.41
),

|R_{5}|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}\,\sinh lx+\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}\,e^{-lx}\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}\,\cosh lx
.
Therefore we obtain for
l\geq l_{0}

D=\phi_{1}(a)+\phi_{2}^{\prime}(a)=2\cosh la\,(1+R_{6})

with
|R_{6}|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}
.

Since, moreover,
\Phi(a)
has the two eigenvalues
e^{\pm\kappa(l)a}
, we get that for
l
large enough,

\displaystyle e^{\kappa(l)a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,D+\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}\,D^{2}-1}=(1+R_{6})\,\cosh la+\sqrt{(1+R_{6})^{2}\,\cosh^{2}la-1}

\displaystyle=(1+R_{6})\,\cosh la+\sqrt{(1+R_{6})^{2}\,\sinh^{2}la+2R_{6}+R_{6}^{2}}

\displaystyle=(1+R_{6})\,\cosh la+(1+R_{6})\,\sinh la\,\sqrt{1+R_{7}}

with
|R_{7}|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}
. Then
R_{8}:=\sqrt{1+R_{7}}-1
also satisfies

|R_{8}|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}
and we find

e^{\kappa(l)a}=e^{la}\,(1+R_{6})+\sinh la\,(1+R_{6})\,R_{8}=e^{la}\,(1+R_{9})

with
|R_{9}|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}
.
Consequently, denoting with
\log
the principal branch of the logarithm
function, we have

\kappa(l)a=la+\log(1+R_{9})+2\pi n{\rm i}

with some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since we assumed that
\mathop{\rm Im}\kappa(l)a\in(-\pi,\pi]
and have found that

|\mathop{\rm Im}\log(1+R_{9})|\leq|\log(1+R_{9})|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}
,
we see that
n=0
and conclude that

|\kappa(l)-l|=\frac{1}{a}\,|\log(1+R_{9})|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}

for
l\geq l_{0}
with some
l_{0}>0
.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Consider the Floquet solutions (
2.35
) of equation (
2.36
).
There exists
l_{0}>0
such that for
l\geq l_{0}
the functions
p_{1},p_{2}
can be chosen so that

\frac{1}{a}\int_{0}^{a}p_{1}(x)\,dx=\frac{1}{a}\int_{0}^{a}p_{2}(x)\,dx=1;

(2.43)

furthermore, there is a constant
C>0
such that

\|p_{1}-1\|_{\infty},\|p_{2}-1\|_{\infty},\|p_{1}^{\prime}\|_{\infty},\|p_{2}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{C}{l}

for all
l\geq l_{0}
.

Proof.

Set
m_{1}:=\kappa(l)
,
m_{2}:=-\kappa(l)
, and let
j\in\{1,2\}
.
Differentiating the Floquet solution
\psi_{j}
in equation (
2.35
) twice and
using the differential equation (
2.36
), we find that
p_{j}
satisfies
the differential equation

p_{j}^{\prime\prime}+2m_{j}p_{j}^{\prime}+(m_{j}^{2}-l^{2}+V)\,p_{j}=0

with periodic boundary conditions.
Let

M:=\frac{1}{a}\int_{0}^{a}p_{j}(x)\,dx.

Then
p_{j}(x)=M+q(x)

(x\in[0,a])
, where

\int_{0}^{a}q(x)\,dx=0.

(2.44)

The function
q
satisfies the differential equation

q^{\prime\prime}+2m_{j}q^{\prime}+(m_{j}^{2}-l^{2})\,q=-V\,q-(m_{j}^{2}-l^{2}+V)\,M,

(2.45)

again with periodic boundary conditions.
Integrating this equation over the interval
[0,a]
and using the periodic
boundary conditions for
q
, we find that

r:=-Vq-(m_{j}^{2}-l^{2}+V)M

has integral

\int_{0}^{a}r(x)\,dx=0.

The homogeneous equation where we set the right-hand side of equation (
2.45
) equal to 0 has the fundamental system

e^{(-m_{j}+l)x}
,
e^{(-m_{j}-l)x}

(x\in[0,a])
, and we can solve the
inhomogeneous equation (
2.45
) by the variation of constants method.
The general solution for
m_{j}^{2}-l^{2}\neq 0
takes the form

q(x)=\frac{1}{2l}\int_{0}^{x}\left(e^{(-m_{j}+l)(x-t)}-e^{(-m_{j}-l)(x-t)}\right)\,r(t)\,dt+c_{1}\,e^{(-m_{j}+l)x}+c_{2}\,e^{(-m_{j}-l)x},\quad x\in[0,a],

and determining
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
from the conditions

q(a)=q(0)
and equation (
2.44
), we find
for all
x\in[0,a]

\displaystyle q(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2l}\int_{0}^{x}\left(-\frac{e^{(l-m_{j})(x-t)}-1}{e^{(l-m_{j})a}-1}+\frac{e^{(-l-m_{j})(x-t)}-1}{e^{(-l-m_{j})a}-1}\right)r(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2l}\int_{x}^{a}\left(\frac{e^{(-l+m_{j})(t-x)}-1}{e^{(-l+m_{j})a}-1}-\frac{e^{(l+m_{j})(t-x)}-1}{e^{(l+m_{j})a}-1}\right)r(t)\,dt;

(2.46)

this solution then also satisfies the boundary condition
q^{\prime}(a)=q^{\prime}(0)
.
If
m_{j}=l
or
m_{j}=-l
, then the formula for
q
is still valid provided the corresponding quotient in the integrands is replaced with the respective limit
\frac{x-t}{a}
or
\frac{t-x}{a}
.

In order to estimate the four quotients appearing in the formula for
q
, we
observe that they all are of the form

\frac{e^{\lambda\zeta}-1}{e^{\zeta}-1}=\frac{\int_{0}^{\lambda}e^{\zeta s}\,ds}{\int_{0}^{1}e^{\zeta s}\,ds},

where
\zeta\in\{\pm(l-m_{j})a,\pm(l+m_{j})a\}
and
\lambda=\frac{|x-t|}{a}\in[0,1]
.
Abbreviating
\alpha:=\mathop{\rm Re}\zeta
,
\beta:=\mathop{\rm Im}\zeta
, we find

\left|\int_{0}^{\lambda}e^{\zeta s}\,ds\right|\leq\int_{0}^{\lambda}e^{\alpha s}\,ds\leq\int_{0}^{1}e^{\alpha s}\,ds,

(2.47)

and

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{1}e^{\zeta s}\,ds\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{1}e^{\alpha s}\,ds+\int_{0}^{1}e^{\alpha s}\left(e^{{\rm i}\beta s}-1\right)\,ds\right|

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{1}e^{\alpha s}\,ds-\int_{0}^{1}e^{\alpha s}\,\left|2i\,e^{{\rm i}\beta s/2}\,\sin\frac{\beta s}{2}\right|\,ds

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{1}e^{\alpha s}\,ds-2\int_{0}^{1}e^{\alpha s}\,\sin\frac{|\beta|\,s}{2}\,ds.

Since

|\beta|=a|\mathop{\rm Im}(\pm l\pm m_{j})|=a|\mathop{\rm Im}m_{j}|\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}

for sufficiently large
l
by Lemma
2.5
,

2\sin\frac{|\beta|\,s}{2}\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}\qquad(s\in[0,1])

and therefore
there is
l_{0}>0
such that

\left|\int_{0}^{1}e^{\zeta s}\,ds\right|\geq\left(1-\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}\right)\int_{0}^{1}e^{\alpha s}\,ds\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\alpha s}\,ds

(2.48)

for all
l\geq l_{0}
.
We thus obtain the following bound for
q
,

\displaystyle\|q\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in[0,a]}\frac{1}{2l}\left(\int_{0}^{x}4\,|r(t)|\,dt+\int_{x}^{a}4\,|r(t)|\,dt\right)\leq\frac{2a}{l}\,\|r\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2a}{l}\,\left(\|V\|_{\infty}\|q\|_{\infty}+|m_{j}^{2}-l^{2}|\,M+\|V\|_{\infty}\,M\right)

and hence

\|q\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\frac{2a}{l}\,M}{1-\frac{2a}{l}\|V\|_{\infty}}\,\left(|m_{j}^{2}-l^{2}|+\|V\|_{\infty}\right)

(2.49)

for
l\geq l_{0}
.
Furthermore, Lemma
2.5
gives

\displaystyle|m_{j}^{2}-l^{2}|

\displaystyle=|\kappa(l)^{2}-l^{2}|=|\kappa(l)-l|\,|\kappa(l)-l+2l|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}\left(\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}+2l\right)\leq\mathord{\rm const},

(2.50)

so by the estimate (
2.49
) we have

\|q\|_{\infty}\leq M\,\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}\quad\text{for}\quad l\geq l_{0}.

(2.51)

We see that
M=0
would imply that
q\equiv 0
and also
p_{j}\equiv 0
,
which would make
\psi_{j}
the trivial solution in contradiction to its definition.
Therefore
M\neq 0
and, after a suitable renormalization of
p_{j}
, we obtain
M=1
. Hence, (
2.51
) gives the desired bound for
p_{j}-1
.

We conclude the proof by estimating
q^{\prime}=p_{j}^{\prime}
. By equation (
2.46
),

\displaystyle q^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2l}\int_{0}^{x}\left(-\frac{l-m}{e^{(l-m)a}-1}\,e^{(l-m)(x-t)}+\frac{-l-m}{e^{(-l-m)a}-1}\,e^{(-l-m)(x-t)}\right)r(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\ +\frac{1}{2l}\int_{x}^{a}\left(-\frac{-l+m}{e^{(-l+m)a}-1}\,e^{(-l+m)(t-x)}+\frac{l+m}{e^{(l+m)a}-1}\,e^{(l+m)(t-x)}\right)r(t)\,dt

and thus, for
l
large enough,

\displaystyle|q^{\prime}(x)|\leq\frac{\|r\|_{\infty}}{2l}\left(\left|\frac{l-m}{e^{(l-m)a}-1}\right|\,\frac{e^{\mathop{\rm Re}(l-m)x}-1}{\mathop{\rm Re}(l-m)}+\left|\frac{-l-m}{e^{(-l-m)a}-1}\right|\,\frac{e^{\mathop{\rm Re}(-l-m)x}-1}{\mathop{\rm Re}(-l-m)}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.+\left|\frac{-l+m}{e^{(-l+m)a}-1}\right|\,\frac{e^{\mathop{\rm Re}(-l+m)(a-x)}-1}{\mathop{\rm Re}(-l+m)}+\left|\frac{l+m}{e^{(l+m)a}-1}\right|\,\frac{e^{\mathop{\rm Re}(l+m)(a-x)}-1}{\mathop{\rm Re}(l+m)}\right).

Each of the four terms appearing in this sum has the form

\left|\frac{\zeta}{e^{\zeta}-1}\right|\,\frac{e^{\lambda\alpha}-1}{\alpha}=\frac{\int_{0}^{\lambda}e^{\alpha s}\,ds}{\left|\int_{0}^{1}e^{\zeta s}\,ds\right|},

where
\zeta\in\{\pm(l-m)a,\pm(l+m)a\}
,
\alpha=\mathop{\rm Re}\zeta
and either

\lambda=\frac{x}{a}\in[0,1]
or
\lambda=1-\frac{x}{a}\in[0,1]
.
Using the estimates (
2.47
) and (
2.48
), we find

\left|\frac{\zeta}{e^{\zeta}-1}\right|\,\frac{e^{\lambda\alpha}-1}{\alpha}\leq 2

for
l\geq l_{0}
.
Therefore, bearing in mind the estimate (
2.50
) and the normalization (
2.43
),

\displaystyle\|q^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|r\|_{\infty}}{2l}\,8\leq\frac{4}{l}\,(\|V\|_{\infty}\,(1+\|q\|_{\infty})+|m_{j}^{2}-l^{2}|\,1)\leq\frac{\mathord{\rm const}}{l}

for
l\geq l_{0}
.
∎

3
The Resolvent Set

To investigate the resolvent set, we need to study the unique solvability of (
2.1
) or, equivalently, (
2.21
). In the following, we work in the transformed variables
u_{1},v,w
.
For ease of notation, in this section we denote the
x_{1}
-variable simply by
x
. The set
{\mathcal{S}}
defined next will be shown to lie in the resolvent set. Recall that
k=k_{2}
if
N=2
and
k=(k_{2},k_{3})
if
N=3
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
N=2,3
. Define
\mathcal{S}
to be the set of all
\omega\in\mathbb{C}
which satisfy i)-iv) below. Here we suppress the
\omega
-dependence of all occurring quantities and note that
\perm_{\pm}(0)
denotes
\perm_{\pm}(x=0,\omega)
.

i)

\omega\in D(\perm)\setminus\Omega
.

ii)

For every
k\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
there exist fundamental systems
\{v^{(1)}_{+},v^{(2)}_{+}\}
and
\{v^{(1)}_{-},v^{(2)}_{-}\}
of the homogeneous equation (
2.15
) on
[0,\infty)
and on
(-\infty,0]
, respectively, such that, for some (possibly
k
-dependent)
\nu_{\pm}>0
and
C_{v,1,\pm},C^{\prime}_{v,1,\pm},C_{v,2,\pm},

C^{\prime}_{v,2,\pm}>0
,

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}|v^{(1)}_{\pm}(x)|\leq C_{v,1,\pm}e^{\nu_{\pm}x},&|v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{\pm}(x)|\leq C^{\prime}_{v,1,\pm}e^{\nu_{\pm}x},\\
|v^{(2)}_{\pm}(x)|\leq C_{v,2,\pm}e^{-\nu_{\pm}x},&|v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{\pm}(x)|\leq C^{\prime}_{v,2,\pm}e^{-\nu_{\pm}x},\end{array}\right\}\penalty\ {\rm on}\penalty\ \mathbb{R}_{\pm}

and moreover,
v^{(1)}_{+}\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),v^{(2)}_{-}\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{-})
, and

d(k):=-v^{(2)}_{+}(0)v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}+v^{(1)}_{-}(0)v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{+}(0)\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}\neq 0.

(3.2)

iii)

For every
k
there exist fundamental systems
\{w^{(1)}_{+},w^{(2)}_{+}\}
and
\{w^{(1)}_{-},w^{(2)}_{-}\}
of the homogeneous equation (
2.16
) on
[0,\infty)
and on
(-\infty,0]
, respectively, such that, for some (possibly
k
-dependent) constants
\widetilde{\nu}_{\pm}>0
and
C_{w,1,\pm},C^{\prime}_{w,1,\pm},

C_{w,2,\pm},C^{\prime}_{w,2,\pm}>0
,

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}|w^{(1)}_{\pm}(x)|\leq C_{w,1,\pm}e^{\widetilde{\nu}_{\pm}x},&|w^{(1)^{\prime}}_{\pm}(x)|\leq C^{\prime}_{w,1,\pm}e^{\widetilde{\nu}_{\pm}x}\\
|w^{(2)}_{\pm}(x)|\leq C_{w,2,\pm}e^{-\widetilde{\nu}_{\pm}x},&|w^{(2)^{\prime}}_{\pm}(x)|\leq C^{\prime}_{w,2,\pm}e^{-\widetilde{\nu}_{\pm}x}\end{array}\right\}\penalty\ {\rm on}\penalty\ \mathbb{R}_{\pm}

and moreover,
w^{(1)}_{+}\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),w^{(2)}_{-}\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{-})
and

\widetilde{d}(k):=-w^{(2)}_{+}(0)w^{(1)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)+w^{(1)}_{-}(0)w^{(2)^{\prime}}_{+}(0)\neq 0.

(3.4)

iv)

With
\tau_{\pm}
and
\widetilde{\tau}_{\pm}
denoting the Wronski determinants

\tau_{\pm}:=\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{\pm}}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{\pm}}(v^{(1)}_{\pm}v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{\pm}-v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{\pm}v^{(2)}_{\pm}),\quad\widetilde{\tau}_{\pm}:=w^{(1)}_{\pm}w^{(2)^{\prime}}_{\pm}-w^{(1)^{\prime}}_{\pm}w^{(2)}_{\pm},

(3.5)

which are constant with respect to
x
(but usually depend on
k
), the following expressions are bounded by a constant independent of
k
:

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{\pm}:=

\displaystyle\frac{C_{v,2,\pm}C_{v,1,\pm}}{|\tau_{\pm}|(1+|k|^{2})\nu_{\pm}},

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}^{+}:=

\displaystyle\frac{C_{v,2,+}}{|d|(1+|k|^{2})\sqrt{\nu_{+}}}\left\{C_{v,1,-}(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{-}}})+\frac{1}{|\tau_{+}|(1+|k|^{2})\sqrt{\nu_{+}}}(C_{v,1,-}C^{\prime}_{v,1,+}+C^{\prime}_{v,1,-}C_{v,1,+})C_{v,2,+}\right\},

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}^{-}:=

\displaystyle\frac{C_{v,1,-}}{|d|(1+|k|^{2})\sqrt{\nu_{-}}}\left\{C_{v,2,+}(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{+}}})+\frac{1}{|\tau_{-}|(1+|k|^{2})\sqrt{\nu_{-}}}(C_{v,2,-}C^{\prime}_{v,2,+}+C^{\prime}_{v,2,-}C_{v,2,+})C_{v,1,-}\right\},

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}^{+}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+|k|^{3})\sqrt{\nu_{+}}}\left[\frac{C^{\prime}_{v,2,+}}{|d|}\left\{C_{v,1,-}(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{-}}})+\frac{1}{|\tau_{+}|(1+|k|^{2})\sqrt{\nu_{+}}}(C_{v,1,-}C^{\prime}_{v,1,+}+C^{\prime}_{v,1,-}C_{v,1,+})C_{v,2,+}\right\}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{C^{\prime}_{v,2,+}C_{v,1,+}+C^{\prime}_{v,1,+}C_{v,2,+}}{|\tau_{+}|\sqrt{\nu_{+}}}\right],

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}^{-}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+|k|^{3})\sqrt{\nu_{-}}}\left[\frac{C^{\prime}_{v,1,-}}{|d|}\left\{C_{v,2,+}(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{+}}})+\frac{1}{|\tau_{-}|(1+|k|^{2})\sqrt{\nu_{-}}}(C^{\prime}_{v,2,+}C_{v,2,-}+C^{\prime}_{v,2,-}C_{v,2,+})C_{v,1,-}\right\}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{C^{\prime}_{v,2,-}C_{v,1,-}+C^{\prime}_{v,1,-}C_{v,2,-}}{|\tau_{-}|\sqrt{\nu_{-}}}\right],

\displaystyle\alpha_{4}^{\pm}:=

\displaystyle\frac{C_{w,2,\pm}C_{w,1,\pm}}{|\widetilde{\tau}_{\pm}|\widetilde{\nu}_{\pm}}(1+|k|),

\displaystyle\alpha_{5}^{+}:=

\displaystyle\frac{C_{w,2,+}(1+|k|)}{|\widetilde{d}|\sqrt{\widetilde{\nu}_{+}}}\left[\frac{C_{w,1,-}C^{\prime}_{w,1,+}+C^{\prime}_{w,1,-}C_{w,1,+}}{|\widetilde{\tau}_{+}|\sqrt{\widetilde{\nu}_{+}}}C_{w,2,+}+\frac{C_{w,1,-}}{\sqrt{\widetilde{\nu}_{-}}}\right],

\displaystyle\alpha_{5}^{-}:=

\displaystyle\frac{C_{w,1,-}(1+|k|)}{|\widetilde{d}|\sqrt{\widetilde{\nu}_{-}}}\left[\frac{C_{w,2,+}}{\sqrt{\widetilde{\nu}_{+}}}+\frac{C_{w,2,-}C^{\prime}_{w,2,+}+C^{\prime}_{w,2,-}C_{w,2,+}}{|\widetilde{\tau}_{-}|\sqrt{\widetilde{\nu}_{-}}}C_{w,1,-}\right],

\displaystyle\alpha_{6}^{+}:=

\displaystyle\frac{C^{\prime}_{w,2,+}}{|\widetilde{d}|\sqrt{\widetilde{\nu}_{+}}}\left\{\frac{C_{w,1,-}C^{\prime}_{w,1,+}+C^{\prime}_{w,1,-}C_{w,1,+}}{|\widetilde{\tau}_{+}|\sqrt{\widetilde{\nu}_{+}}}C_{w,2,+}+\frac{C_{w,1,-}}{\sqrt{\widetilde{\nu}_{-}}}\right\}+\frac{C^{\prime}_{w,2,+}C_{w,1,+}+C^{\prime}_{w,1,+}C_{w,2,+}}{|\widetilde{\tau}_{+}|\widetilde{\nu}_{+}},

\displaystyle\alpha_{6}^{-}:=

\displaystyle\frac{C^{\prime}_{w,1,-}}{|\widetilde{d}|\sqrt{\widetilde{\nu}_{-}}}\left\{\frac{C_{w,2,+}}{\sqrt{\widetilde{\nu}_{+}}}+\frac{C_{w,2,-}C^{\prime}_{w,2,+}+C^{\prime}_{w,2,-}C_{w,2,+}}{|\widetilde{\tau}_{-}|\sqrt{\widetilde{\nu}_{-}}}C_{w,1,-}\right\}+\frac{C^{\prime}_{w,2,-}C_{w,1,-}+C^{\prime}_{w,1,-}C_{w,2,-}}{|\widetilde{\tau}_{-}|\widetilde{\nu}_{-}}.

Note that in the special cases of homogeneous media, Sec.
3.1
, and periodic media, Sec
3.2
, we can give a much simpler description of the set
\mathcal{S}
.

We next show that the set
\mathcal{S}
lies in the resolvent set of
\mathcal{L}
.

Theorem 3.2
.

We have

\mathcal{S}\subset\rho(\mathcal{L})
.

Proof.

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{S}
, i.e., i), ii), iii), iv) in Definition
3.1
hold true. By Lemmas
2.1
and
2.2
, we have to prove that the inhomogeneous system (
2.15
), (
2.16
) has a unique solution
(v,w)
such that
(u_{1},v,w)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
with
u_{1}
defined by (
2.17
) and that
\|(u_{1},v,w)\|_{L^{2}}\leq c\|r\|_{L^{2}}
with
c
independent of
r
. The case
k=0
can be left out because only
L^{2}
-estimates with respect to
k
are required and the set
\{(x_{1},k)\text{ with }k=0\}
has measure
0
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Hence, we assume
k\neq 0
.
Let
s
denote the right-hand side of (
2.15
). With
\{v^{(1)}_{+},v^{(2)}_{+}\}
and
\{v^{(1)}_{-},v^{(2)}_{-}\}
denoting fundamental systems for (
2.15
) given by ii), we first recall that the Wronski determinants
\tau_{\pm}
defined in (
3.5
) are constant in
x
and depend only on
k
.

By the variation of constants formula we get a particular solution
v^{{\rm part}}
of (
2.15
) by

\begin{array}[]{l}v^{{\rm part}}_{+}(x)=-\frac{v^{(2)}_{+}(x)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{0}^{x}v^{(1)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt-\frac{v^{(1)}_{+}(x)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}v^{(2)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt\quad(x>0),\\
v^{{\rm part}}_{-}(x)=-\frac{v^{(2)}_{-}(x)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{x}v^{(1)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt-\frac{v^{(1)}_{-}(x)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{x}^{0}v^{(2)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt\quad(x<0).\end{array}

(3.6)

By ii)

\begin{array}[]{lcl}|v^{{\rm part}}_{+}(x)|&\leq&\frac{C_{v,2,+}C_{v,1,+}}{|\tau_{+}|}\left[e^{-\nu_{+}x}\int\limits_{0}^{x}e^{\nu_{+}t}|s(t)|dt+e^{\nu_{+}x}\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}e^{-\nu_{+}t}|s(t)|dt\right]\quad(x>0),\\
|v^{{\rm part}}_{-}(x)|&\leq&\frac{C_{v,2,-}C_{v,1,-}}{|\tau_{-}|}\left[e^{-\nu_{-}x}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{x}e^{\nu_{-}t}|s(t)|dt+e^{\nu_{-}x}\int\limits_{x}^{0}e^{-\nu_{-}t}|s(t)|dt\right]\quad(x<0),\end{array}

whence Lemma 4.7 of
[
7
]
(and its natural extension to
\mathbb{R}_{-}
) gives

v^{{\rm part}}_{\pm}(\cdot,k)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm}),\penalty\ \|v^{{\rm part}}_{\pm}(\cdot,k)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm})}\leq\frac{2C_{v,2,\pm}C_{v,1,\pm}}{|\tau_{\pm}|\nu_{\pm}}\|s(\cdot,k)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm})}.

(3.7)

From now on
c>0
denotes a generic
k
-independent constant.

By (
2.15
) we have

\|s(\cdot,k)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm})}\leq\frac{c}{1+|k|^{2}}\left\|r(\cdot,k)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm})^{3}}\penalty\ {\rm for\penalty\ a.e.}\penalty\ k\in\mathbb{R}^{N-1}.

(3.8)

Hence, the boundedness of
\alpha_{1}^{\pm}
in assumption iv) gives

v^{{\rm part}}_{\pm}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm}^{N}),\penalty\ \|v^{{\rm part}}_{\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm}^{N})}\leq c\left\|r\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm}^{N})^{3}}.

(3.9)

Since
v^{(1)}_{+}\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),v^{(2)}_{-}\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{-})
by ii), the general solution in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm})
of equation (
2.15
) is given by

\begin{array}[]{ll}v_{+}(x)=A_{+}v^{(2)}_{+}(x)+v_{+}^{{\rm part}}(x)&(x>0),\\
v_{-}(x)=A_{-}v^{(1)}_{-}(x)+v_{-}^{{\rm part}}(x)&(x<0),\end{array}

(3.10)

with (
k
-dependent) constants
A_{+},A_{-}\in\mathbb{C}
. The condition
\llbracket v\rrbracket=0
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
requires
v_{+}(0)=v_{-}(0)
, i.e.

\displaystyle A_{+}v^{(2)}_{+}(0)-A_{-}v^{(1)}_{-}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v^{{\rm part}}_{-}(0)-v^{{\rm part}}_{+}(0)

(3.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{v^{(1)}_{+}(0)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}v^{(2)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt-\frac{v^{(2)}_{-}(0)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{0}v^{(1)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt.

Before analyzing this further, we first compute, using (
3.10
) and (
3.6
),

\begin{array}[]{l}v^{\prime}_{+}(x)=A_{+}v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{+}(x)-\frac{v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{+}(x)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{0}^{x}v^{(1)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt-\frac{v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{+}(x)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}v^{(2)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt\penalty\ (x>0),\\
v^{\prime}_{-}(x)=A_{-}v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{-}(x)-\frac{v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{-}(x)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{x}v^{(1)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt-\frac{v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{-}(x)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{x}^{0}v^{(2)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt\penalty\ (x<0),\end{array}

(3.12)

which, by (
2.17
), gives

\begin{array}[]{l}u_{1,+}(x)=\frac{1}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(x)}\left\{r_{1}(x)-{\rm i}|k|\left[A_{+}v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{+}(x)-\frac{v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{+}(x)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{0}^{x}v^{(1)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt-\frac{v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{+}(x)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}v^{(2)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt\right]\right\}\penalty\ (x>0),\\
u_{1,-}(x)=\frac{1}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(x)}\left\{r_{1}(x)-{\rm i}|k|\left[A_{-}v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{-}(x)-\frac{v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{-}(x)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{x}v^{(1)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt-\frac{v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{-}(x)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{x}^{0}v^{(2)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt\right]\right\}\penalty\ (x<0).\end{array}

(3.13)

The condition
\llbracket\perm u_{1}\rrbracket=0
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\omega}
therefore requires

\displaystyle\frac{\perm_{+}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}\left\{r_{1}(0)-{\rm i}|k|\left[A_{+}v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{+}(0)-\frac{v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{+}(0)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}v^{(2)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}\left\{r_{1}(0)-{\rm i}|k|\left[A_{-}v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)-\frac{v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{0}v^{(1)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt\right]\right\},

i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{\perm_{+}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{+}(0)A_{+}-\frac{\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)A_{-}\ =\ \left[\frac{\perm_{+}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}-\frac{\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}\right]\frac{r_{1}(0)}{{\rm i}|k|}

(3.14)

\displaystyle+\frac{\perm_{+}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}\frac{v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{+}(0)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}v^{(2)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt-\frac{\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}\frac{v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{0}v^{(1)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt.

Equations (
3.11
) and (
3.14
) together form a
2\times 2
-system for
(A_{+},A_{-})^{\top}
, the determinant of which is

-v^{(2)}_{+}(0)v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)\frac{\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}+v^{(1)}_{-}(0)v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{+}(0)\frac{\perm_{+}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}=\frac{d}{\omega^{2}}

(3.15)

with
d
defined in (
3.2
). The determinant is non-zero by assumption ii). Thus,
A_{+}
and
A_{-}
are uniquely given by

\displaystyle A_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{d}\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)\left[\frac{v^{(1)}_{+}(0)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}v^{(2)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt-\frac{v^{(2)}_{-}(0)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{0}v^{(1)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt\right]

(3.16)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{d}v^{(1)}_{-}(0)\Bigg\{\left[\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}-\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}\right]\frac{r_{1}(0)}{{\rm i}|k|}

\displaystyle+\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}\frac{v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{+}(0)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}v^{(2)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt-\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}\frac{v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{0}v^{(1)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\frac{|k|(\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0))}{(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0))(|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0))}\cdot\frac{1}{{\rm i}}v^{(1)}_{-}(0)r_{1}(0)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{d\tau_{+}}\left[\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}v^{(1)}_{-}(0)v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{+}(0)-\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)v^{(1)}_{+}(0)\right]\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}v^{(2)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{d\tau_{-}}\underbrace{\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}\left[v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)v^{(2)}_{-}(0)-v^{(1)}_{-}(0)v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)\right]}_{=-\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{0}v^{(1)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt,

\displaystyle A_{-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{d}\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{+}(0)\left[\frac{v^{(1)}_{+}(0)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}v^{(2)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt-\frac{v^{(2)}_{-}(0)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{0}v^{(1)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt\right]

(3.17)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{d}v^{(2)}_{+}(0)\Bigg\{\left[\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}-\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}\right]\frac{r_{1}(0)}{{\rm i}|k|}

\displaystyle+\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{+}(0)}\frac{v^{(1)^{\prime}}_{+}(0)}{\tau_{+}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}v^{(2)}_{+}(t)s(t)dt-\frac{\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}{|k|^{2}-\omega^{2}\perm_{-}(0)}\frac{v^{(2)^{\prime}}_{-}(0)}{\tau_{-}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{0}v^{(1)}_{-}(t)s(t)dt\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=
